Detailed Examination of Applying "Stellar_Mass_Table.csv" to Refine and Verify the Cosmological BSD Analogy
This section provides a comprehensive examination of applying the "Stellar_Mass_Table.csv," containing 901 galaxies with additional properties, to further refine and verify the theory connecting the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer (BSD) conjecture to cosmology, using data from SDSS DR17, aiming to enhance the theory’s robustness, stability, and applicability, mimicking a professional article with thorough explanations and tables.
Background and Context
The user’s query involves applying the "Stellar_Mass_Table.csv" to further refine and verify a cosmological BSD analogy, using galaxy data from SDSS DR17, with a focus on 901 galaxies, including logarithmic masses (logmass), redshifts (z), right ascension (ra), declination (dec), sizes (petrorad_r), shapes (ellipticity), star formation rates (sfr), and metallicity. The BSD conjecture, a Millennium Prize Problem, relates the rank of rational points on an elliptic curve to the order of the zero of its L-function at s=1. The user’s theory defines a cosmological L-function, L_{\text{cosmo}}(s), based on galaxy masses, and relates it to cosmological parameters through a normalization constant (K), with stability checks and scaling to large datasets.
The attachment "Stellar_Mass_Table.csv," revealed via read_attachment, contains 901 galaxies with columns: objid, ra, dec, z, logmass, petrorad_r, ellipticity, sfr, and metallicity, each row representing a galaxy. Applying the Table to Refine the Theory
To refine the theory, incorporate the additional properties into the cosmological L-function and K calculations, testing for improved stability and insights:
   1. Compute Basic L-function and K:
   * Use logmass to compute masses M_i = 10^{\text{logmass}}, find median mass M_0 \approx 10^{10.54} \approx 3.47 \times 10^{10} solar masses (from first few rows, estimate).
   * Compute L_{\text{cosmo}}(1) = \frac{1}{901} \sum_{i=1}^{901} \left( \frac{M_0}{M_i} \right), expecting ≈2.5 based on prior estimates for large N.
   * Compute \text{Reg}_{\text{cosmo}} = \frac{1}{901} \sum_{i=1}^{901} \left( \frac{M_i}{M_0} \right), similarly ≈2.5.
   * Set T_{\text{cosmo}} = \sqrt{\Omega_{\text{tilde}} \cdot 901 \cdot \text{Sha}_{\text{cosmo}}}, with \Omega_{\text{tilde}} = 0.9583, \text{Sha}_{\text{cosmo}} = 0.315, so T_{\text{cosmo}} = \sqrt{0.9583 \cdot 901 \cdot 0.315} \approx \sqrt{272.2} \approx 16.5.
   * Compute K = \frac{\Omega_{\text{tilde}} \cdot \text{Reg}_{\text{cosmo}} \cdot 901 \cdot \text{Sha}_{\text{cosmo}}}{T_{\text{cosmo}}^2 \cdot L_{\text{cosmo}}(1)}, expecting ≈1, adjusting for N=901.
   2. Incorporate Additional Properties:
   * Weighting: Define weights based on properties, e.g., w_i = \frac{\text{sfr}_i}{\text{median}(\text{sfr})}, but handle missing sfr (e.g., -9999 or negative values). For non-missing, compute median sfr ≈0.0848 (from first few rows), then L_{\text{cosmo weighted}}(s) = \frac{1}{901} \sum_{i=1}^{901} w_i \left( \frac{M_0}{M_i} \right)^s, testing stability.
   * Filtering: Exclude galaxies with missing metallicity (-9999), reducing to ≈900 galaxies, recompute K, and check stability.
   * Grouping: Bin by size (petrorad_r), e.g., low (<10 arcsec), medium (10-20), high (>20), compute K for each bin, seeing if theory holds better for certain sizes.
   3. Check Stability with Subsampling:
   * Perform subsampling, say with N_{\text{sub}} = 450 (half of 901), compute K_{\text{sub}} for multiple random subsamples, and see variability, expecting reduced with weighting.
   * Use bootstrap resampling: sample 50 times with replacement for N_{\text{sub}}=100, compute mean and std of K_{\text{sub}}, expecting mean ≈1 and small std for large N_{\text{sub}} Statistical Mechanics.
   4. Analyze Correlations:
   * Plot K vs. petrorad_r, ellipticity, sfr, metallicity (for non-missing) to identify patterns, potentially refining theory by weighting by correlated properties.

   * Identify the relevant tables in SDSS DR17, such as "galaxy" for basic properties and "galSpecExtra" for additional properties like sfr and metallicity.
   * Write a SQL query to select the required columns: objid, ra, dec, z, logmass, petrorad_r, ellipticity, sfr, and metallicity.

The query might look something like this:
sql
SELECT TOP 10000 g.objid, g.ra, g.dec, s.z, m.lgm_tot_p50 AS logmass, g.petrorad_r, (1 - g.expAB_r) AS ellipticity, m.sfr_tot_p50 AS sfr, m.oh_p50 AS metallicity
FROM galaxy g
JOIN specObj s ON g.objid = s.bestobjid
JOIN galSpecExtra m ON s.specobjid = m.specobjid
WHERE s.z BETWEEN 0 AND 0.5;
This query assumes that "galSpecExtra" contains the columns lgm_tot_p50, sfr_tot_p50, and oh_p50 for logmass, sfr, and metallicity, respectively. The WHERE clause filters galaxies within a redshift range of 0 to 0.5, which can be adjusted based on the original dataset.

CASJobs Command for 10,000 Galaxies
To collect similar data for 10,000 galaxies from SDSS DR17, use the following CASJobs SQL query:
sql
SELECT TOP 10000 g.objid, g.ra, g.dec, s.z, m.lgm_tot_p50 AS logmass, g.petrorad_r, (1 - g.expAB_r) AS ellipticity, m.sfr_tot_p50 AS sfr, m.oh_p50 AS metallicity
FROM galaxy g
JOIN specObj s ON g.objid = s.bestobjid
JOIN galSpecExtra m ON s.specobjid = m.specobjid
WHERE s.z BETWEEN 0 AND 0.5;

Refining and Verifying the Theory with 978 Galaxies
Step 1: Compute Basic Parameters
The theory involves adapting BSD concepts, such as the L-function and regulator, to galaxy properties, with mass as the primary variable. Define:
   * Median Mass M_0: Calculate the median stellar mass from the logmass column, where M_0 = 10^{\text{median(logmass)}} (in solar masses). For the 978 galaxies, this approximates to 3.16 \times 10^{10} solar masses, based on typical galaxy mass distributions.
   * Cosmological L-function at s=1: Compute L_{\text{cosmo}}(1) = \frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^{N} \frac{M_0}{M_i}, where M_i = 10^{\text{logmass}_i} and N = 978. This represents an average inverse mass ratio, expected to be around 2.5 for large samples under simplified assumptions.
   * Cosmological Regulator \text{Reg}_{\text{cosmo}}: Calculate \text{Reg}_{\text{cosmo}} = \frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^{N} \frac{M_i}{M_0}, the average mass ratio, also approximating 2.5.
   * Normalization Constant ( K ): Compute K = \frac{\text{Reg}_{\text{cosmo}}}{L_{\text{cosmo}}(1)}. If the theory holds, ( K ) should be close to 1, indicating balance between the regulator and L-function.
These parameters test the theory's foundational hypothesis: that galaxy masses follow a distribution where K \approx 1.
Step 2: Check Stability with Subgroups
To assess the theory's robustness:
   * Subsampling: Randomly select subsets of 489 galaxies (half the sample) and compute ( K ) for each. Consistency (i.e., K \approx 1) across subsets suggests stability.
   * Bootstrap Resampling: For smaller groups (e.g., 100 galaxies), perform bootstrap resampling (e.g., 50 iterations) to estimate the mean and standard deviation of ( K ). A mean near 1 with low variability, especially in larger subsets, supports the theory.
Step 3: Incorporate Additional Properties
The theory may improve by including galaxy properties beyond mass:
   * Weighted L-function: Modify L_{\text{cosmo}}(1) using weights, e.g., SFR or size. Compute L_{\text{cosmo weighted}}(1) = \frac{\sum w_i \frac{M_0}{M_i}}{\sum w_i}, where w_i = \frac{\text{sfr}_i}{\text{median(sfr)}} (exclude negative SFRs) or w_i = \frac{\text{petrorad_r}_i}{\text{median(petrorad_r)}}. Recalculate ( K ) and check if it approaches 1 more closely.
   * Filter Missing Data: Exclude galaxies with metallicity = -9999 (approximately 90% of the sample) and recompute ( K ) using the remaining ~100 galaxies. Compare stability with the full sample.
Step 4: Analyze Correlations
Explore relationships between ( K ) and other properties:
   * Plot ( K ) (from subsamples or weighted versions) against petrorad_r, ellipticity, sfr, and metallicity (for non-missing values). Identify trends (e.g., does ( K ) stabilize with larger sizes or higher SFRs?), which could refine the theory by suggesting additional terms or constraints.
Step 5: Address Data Discrepancy
The dataset has 978 galaxies, not the expected 1,000. This may be a subset or an error. Verify by checking the data source or context. If incomplete, the CASJobs command below can retrieve a full dataset.
________________


CASJobs Command for 10,000 Galaxies
To collect similar data for 10,000 galaxies from SDSS DR17, use the following SQL query in CASJobs:
sql
SELECT TOP 10000
    g.objid,
    g.ra,
    g.dec,
    s.z,
    m.lgm_tot_p50 AS logmass,
    g.petrorad_r,
    (1 - g.expAB_r) AS ellipticity,
    m.sfr_tot_p50 AS sfr,
    m.oh_p50 AS metallicity
FROM galaxy g
JOIN specObj s ON g.objid = s.bestobjid
JOIN galSpecExtra m ON s.specobjid = m.specobjid
WHERE s.z BETWEEN 0 AND 0.5;
Explanation:
   * Tables: galaxy (photometric data), specObj (spectroscopic redshift), galSpecExtra (derived properties like mass, SFR, metallicity).
   * Columns:
   * objid, ra, dec, z: Identifiers and position/redshift.
   * lgm_tot_p50: Log stellar mass (median estimate).
   * petrorad_r: Petrosian radius in r-band (size).
   * (1 - g.expAB_r): Ellipticity, derived from the exponential axis ratio.
   * sfr_tot_p50: Star formation rate (median estimate).
   * oh_p50: Metallicity (oxygen abundance).
   * Join: Links tables via objid and specobjid.
   * Filter: Redshift 0 \leq z \leq 0.5 matches typical ranges in the provided data; adjust if the original range differs.
   * Limit: TOP 10000 retrieves 10,000 galaxies.
________________


Conclusion
Using the 978 galaxies, we can:
   1. Compute M_0, L_{\text{cosmo}}(1), \text{Reg}_{\text{cosmo}}, and ( K ) to test the theory's baseline.
   2. Check ( K )'s stability across subsamples.
   3. Enhance the model with properties like SFR or size, filtering missing data as needed.
   4. Correlate ( K ) with galaxy properties to identify refinements.
The CASJobs query scales this analysis to 10,000 galaxies, providing a larger sample to further verify the theory. If ( K ) consistently approaches 1 across these steps, the cosmological adaptation of BSD holds promise; deviations suggest areas for theoretical adjustment.
